========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Um, well, I guess I came in because work has been really overwhelming lately. I'm an event planner, and recently I've had to do a lot more front-facing stuff. Like last week, I was running this massive corporate mixer, and the catering was an absolute nightmare. They brought the completely wrong vegan options—which, people get *so* mad about, and I get it, but it wasn't even my fault! The chef literally didn't read my emails. 

But anyway, because I'm running these things now, I have to be in front of people a lot, and they take all these promotional photos. And... I just can't stop staring at this one part of my face in the pictures. It looks so incredibly uneven. It's all I can look at. I've tried pointing it out to my friends and my coworkers, and they always say they barely see anything or that I'm crazy, but it's glaringly obvious to me. It just ruins the whole picture. Kind of like how one bad appetizer tray ruined that whole mixer, you know? But yeah, the photos are... they're really bothering me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, it's definitely not just when I see the final pictures. Like, before I even send out any event photos or right before I have to hop on a vendor meeting... Oh, actually, speaking of vendors, I have this one florist, Brenda, who is honestly *so* difficult to work with. She always tries to push these really aggressive neon lilies when I specifically ask for soft pastel peonies. Like, peonies are just so much more elegant for a spring mixer, you know? Why would you want neon floral arrangements at a corporate networking event? It just looks cheap, and I'm the one who gets blamed if the aesthetic is off. 

But anyway, what was I saying? Right, the unevenness. So before I meet with people like Brenda, or before I send out pictures, I’ll just sit there and keep checking my face in my phone camera. I'll retake photos of myself over and over, or I'll try to tilt my head a certain way to see if I can hide it. Sometimes I'll even pull aside a coworker—usually Jessica, she’s the one who had to help me deal with that awful vegan catering mess last week—and I'll ask her if I look weird. And honestly, doing all that... the checking and asking and tilting... it ends up eating up so much of my time that I actually fall behind on my planning stuff. Which just makes everything more stressful because then vendors start emailing me in all caps asking for updates.

Um, the most distressing? I don't know if I'd say it's the *most* distressing thing. I mean, it's definitely up there, but... honestly, right now, the most distressing thing is probably this whole seating chart fiasco for the gala. 

I was up until 2:00 AM last night trying to figure out where to put the VIP sponsors. Because, okay, so the main sponsor is this tech company, right? And their CEO absolutely hates being seated near the kitchen doors—which I totally get, the swinging doors are super distracting and the waitstaff is always rushing by. But the only other premium tables are near the stage, and the *other* big sponsor, this local real estate firm, specifically requested stage-adjacent seating. And they have this weird rivalry going on, so I can't put them next to each other. It's like a giant puzzle, and if I mess it up, they might pull their funding for next year. I literally had a stress dream about it where all the tables were just floating in the air. 

So, yeah... I don't know if my face is the *most* distressing thing. It's just... I'm not really sure how to rank it with everything else going on.

I’m not really sure how to, like, measure that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I don't know if I'd say it's the *most* distressing thing. And their CEO absolutely hates being seated near the kitchen doors—which I totally get, the swinging doors are super distracting and the waitstaff is always rushing by. But the only other premium tables are near the stage, and the *other* big sponsor, this local real estate firm, specifically requested stage-adjacent seating. And they have this weird rivalry going on, so I can't put them next to each other. It's like a giant puzzle, and if I mess it up, they might pull their funding for next year. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, when I'm not looking at pictures? I don't really know. I guess I haven't really kept track of when I think about it. It just sort of pops into my head sometimes, but I'm not sure if there's a specific pattern to it. 

Actually, speaking of pictures, the photographer for the gala just sent over his shot list, and I am so frustrated. He completely ignored my notes about the lighting in the foyer. I specifically told him we need warm fill lights because the marble floors bounce the overheads in this really harsh, unflattering way. And he's like, "Oh, I'll just fix it in post." Like, no! You can't just fix terrible lighting in post when the client is expecting high-end event photography for their annual report. I swear, half my job is just repeating myself to people who think they know better. I even sent him a mood board with exact references for the shadows. It's just exhausting having to micromanage professionals, you know? 

But, um, to answer your question... yeah, I'm not really sure if I think about my face when I'm not getting ready or whatever. I haven't really noticed.

Um, every day? I don't know... maybe? I really haven't been keeping a tally or anything. It's just hard to say. 

Like, my days just blur together lately anyway because of this floral crisis. I had to spend three hours yesterday on the phone with wholesalers trying to find a substitute that doesn't look cheap. White ranunculus is fine, I guess, but it doesn't have that striking dark center that the anemones have, which was supposed to tie in with the black velvet table runners. It just throws off the whole visual balance of the room. It’s so stressful when you have this perfect vision in your head and people just drop the ball. 

But yeah, about the everyday thing... I really couldn't tell you for sure. I just don't know.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, do other people notice it? Well, that's actually kind of why I'm here. Because ever since I got promoted and I have to be the one standing at the front of the room, directing the staff and greeting the clients, I just... I can't stop staring at this one uneven part of my face whenever I see event photos. But when I bring it up to my friends or my coworkers, they always say they barely see anything, or that I'm crazy for even noticing it. 

Which is exactly what the catering manager said to me yesterday when I told him the fondant on the dessert bites was a shade too yellow! He was like, "Nobody is going to notice that, it's just white." And I had to explain to him that there is a huge difference between ivory and buttercream, especially when they're sitting on a stark white porcelain tray. It just looks like they've gone bad or something. I mean, the lighting at the venue is already going to wash things out, so if the undertones are wrong, the whole dessert table is going to look cheap. I had to literally hold up a pantone swatch to the petit fours to prove my point. People just do not pay attention to detail, and it drives me insane. 

But yeah, with my face... people say they don't really see it, even though I can't stop looking at it in pictures.

They brought the completely wrong vegan options—which, people get *so* mad about, and I get it, but it wasn't even my fault! The chef literally didn't read my emails. \n\nBut anyway, because I'm running these things now, I have to be in front of people a lot, and they take all these promotional photos. And... I just can't stop staring at this one part of my face in the pictures. It looks so incredibly uneven. It's all I can look at. I've tried pointing it out to my friends and my coworkers, and they always say they barely see anything or that I'm crazy, but it's glaringly obvious to me. It just ruins the whole picture. Kind of like how one bad appetizer tray ruined that whole mixer, you know? Like, before I even send out any event photos or right before I have to hop on a vendor meeting... Oh, actually, speaking of vendors, I have this one florist, Brenda, who is honestly *so* difficult to work with. She always tries to push these really aggressive neon lilies when I specifically ask for soft pastel peonies. Like, peonies are just so much more elegant for a spring mixer, you know? Why would you want neon floral arrangements at a corporate networking event? It just looks cheap, and I'm the one who gets blamed if the aesthetic is off. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, the unevenness. So before I meet with people like Brenda, or before I send out pictures, I’ll just sit there and keep checking my face in my phone camera. I'll retake photos of myself over and over, or I'll try to tilt my head a certain way to see if I can hide it. Sometimes I'll even pull aside a coworker—usually Jessica, she’s the one who had to help me deal with that awful vegan catering mess last week—and I'll ask her if I look weird. And honestly, doing all that... the checking and asking and tilting... it ends up eating up so much of my time that I actually fall behind on my planning stuff. Which just makes everything more stressful because then vendors start emailing me in all caps asking for updates.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about the unevenness of your face affect your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? I don't know, maybe a little? I mean, I guess I don't really go out as much as I used to. My friend Sarah was just asking me to go to this brunch place downtown this weekend. They do this bottomless mimosa thing, which sounds fun, but the last time we went to a brunch spot, the waitstaff was so incredibly disorganized. Like, they seated us and then didn't bring water for twenty minutes. And as an event planner, I just can't turn that part of my brain off, you know? I'm sitting there watching their whole floor plan fail, and I'm just like, \"If you just moved the host stand to the left, this wouldn't be a bottleneck!\" \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I guess I haven't really noticed it affecting my friends or family too much. I mean, I still talk to them. It's just hard to find time anyway when work is so crazy. Like, my mom called me yesterday right in the middle of a venue walkthrough, and I had to just let it go to voicemail because the lighting guy was trying to tell me we couldn't do the uplighting I wanted. Which was ridiculous, because I specifically checked the power grid specs before we booked the space. So, yeah, I'm not really sure about the unevenness thing outside of work.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when you think about the unevenness of your face?\n\nPatient: Um, distressed? I’m not really sure how to, like, measure that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I guess it's just sort of mixed in with all the other stress I have going on. \n\nLike... actually, speaking of stress, I am currently dealing with the absolute worst vendor situation for this winter gala I'm putting together. It's this guy, DJ Marcus. I've worked with him before, and usually he's totally fine, but he just completely ghosted me after I sent the deposit. His phone goes straight to voicemail now. And it is driving me crazy because I had this very specific 1920s jazz-fusion vibe planned for the cocktail hour, which is crucial because the venue has these gorgeous original Art Deco chandeliers. It took me weeks to secure that space. If I have to hire some last-minute amateur who just blasts top 40 club hits while people are trying to eat mini beef Wellingtons... it’s going to completely destroy the entire ambiance. It is just so hard to find reliable people who actually understand the aesthetic I'm going for. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, about my face. And it is driving me crazy because I had this very specific 1920s jazz-fusion vibe planned for the cocktail hour, which is crucial because the venue has these gorgeous original Art Deco chandeliers. It took me weeks to secure that space. If I have to hire some last-minute amateur who just blasts top 40 club hits while people are trying to eat mini beef Wellingtons... it’s going to completely destroy the entire ambiance. It is just so hard to find reliable people who actually understand the aesthetic I'm going for. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, about my face. Yeah, I don't know exactly how distressed I am about it. I'm not really sure. I just know it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that thinking about the unevenness of your face is one of the most distressing things you're dealing with right now?\n\nPatient: Um, the most distressing? I don't know if I'd say it's the *most* distressing thing. I mean, it's definitely up there, but... honestly, right now, the most distressing thing is probably this whole seating chart fiasco for the gala. \n\nI was up until 2:00 AM last night trying to figure out where to put the VIP sponsors. And their CEO absolutely hates being seated near the kitchen doors—which I totally get, the swinging doors are super distracting and the waitstaff is always rushing by. But the only other premium tables are near the stage, and the *other* big sponsor, this local real estate firm, specifically requested stage-adjacent seating. And they have this weird rivalry going on, so I can't put them next to each other. It's like a giant puzzle, and if I mess it up, they might pull their funding for next year. I literally had a stress dream about it where all the tables were just floating in the air. \n\nSo, yeah... I don't know if my face is the *most* distressing thing. It's just... I don't really know. I guess I haven't really kept track of when I think about it. It just sort of pops into my head sometimes, but I'm not sure if there's a specific pattern to it. \n\nActually, speaking of pictures, the photographer for the gala just sent over his shot list, and I am so frustrated. He completely ignored my notes about the lighting in the foyer. I specifically told him we need warm fill lights because the marble floors bounce the overheads in this really harsh, unflattering way. And he's like, \"Oh, I'll just fix it in post.\" Like, no! You can't just fix terrible lighting in post when the client is expecting high-end event photography for their annual report. I swear, half my job is just repeating myself to people who think they know better. I even sent him a mood board with exact references for the shadows. It's just exhausting having to micromanage professionals, you know? \n\nBut, um, to answer your question... yeah, I'm not really sure if I think about my face when I'm not getting ready or whatever. It just throws off the whole visual balance of the room. It’s so stressful when you have this perfect vision in your head and people just drop the ball. \n\nBut yeah, about the everyday thing... Um, is it actually there? I mean... I don't really know. Like, obviously I see it, but I guess I can't say for sure if it's, like, objectively there the way I see it or not. I'm just not sure. 

Honestly, trying to figure out what's real and what's just me overthinking is basically my entire life right now. Like with the seating chart for the VIP dinner next week. I spent four hours last night staring at this massive spreadsheet, trying to figure out if putting the regional director next to the keynote speaker was a subtle power move or just a logistical necessity because of the dietary restrictions. He was like, \"Nobody is going to notice that, it's just white.\" And I had to explain to him that there is a huge difference between ivory and buttercream, especially when they're sitting on a stark white porcelain tray. It just looks like they've gone bad or something. I mean, the lighting at the venue is already going to wash things out, so if the undertones are wrong, the whole dessert table is going to look cheap. I had to literally hold up a pantone swatch to the petit fours to prove my point. People just do not pay attention to detail, and it drives me insane. \n\nBut yeah, with my face... people say they don't really see it, even though I can't stop looking at it in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the unevenness of your face is actually there, even if other people don't notice it?\n\nPatient: Um, is it actually there? I'm just not sure. \n\nHonestly, trying to figure out what's real and what's just me overthinking is basically my entire life right now. Like with the seating chart for the VIP dinner next week. I spent four hours last night staring at this massive spreadsheet, trying to figure out if putting the regional director next to the keynote speaker was a subtle power move or just a logistical necessity because of the dietary restrictions. The keynote is strictly vegan, but the regional director is doing keto, and the venue's kitchen is so small I had to group the special meals near the service doors so the waitstaff doesn't cause a traffic jam. Um, when I'm not looking at pictures? I don't really know... I guess I'm not sure. It's hard to say. 

Actually, speaking of pictures, you wouldn't believe the absolute nightmare I'm dealing with right now regarding the photo booth vendor for this weekend's corporate retreat. The vendor—who I specifically vetted, by the way—sent over the digital proofs for the printed photo strips yesterday, and they used the complete wrong hex code for the company's logo. It was supposed to be this very specific, cool-toned slate gray, and it printed out looking like this muddy, warm taupe color. I was furious. I mean, branding is literally everything for this client. If the CEO sees taupe on the photo strips, he's going to lose his mind. 

So I had to drop everything and drive all the way out to their warehouse in the middle of rush hour traffic just to look at their printer settings in person. And it turns out they were just using cheap, third-party ink cartridges to cut costs! I had to threaten to pull their contract entirely and blast them on Yelp just to get them to recalibrate the machine and use the right ink. It is just so exhausting having to micromanage grown adults who can't even do the one job they were hired to do. 

But, um, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, my face without pictures. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I just don't know.

But when I bring it up to my friends or my coworkers, they always say they barely see anything, or that I'm crazy for even noticing it. \n\nWhich is exactly what the catering manager said to me yesterday when I told him the fondant on the dessert bites was a shade too yellow! He was like, \"Nobody is going to notice that, it's just white.\" And I had to explain to him that there is a huge difference between ivory and buttercream, especially when they're sitting on a stark white porcelain tray. It just looks like they've gone bad or something. I mean, the lighting at the venue is already going to wash things out, so if the undertones are wrong, the whole dessert table is going to look cheap. I had to literally hold up a pantone swatch to the petit fours to prove my point. People just do not pay attention to detail, and it drives me insane. \n\nBut yeah, with my face... people say they don't really see it, even though I can't stop looking at it in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the unevenness of your face is actually there, even if other people don't notice it?\n\nPatient: Um, is it actually there? I mean... I don't really know. Like, obviously I see it, but I guess I can't say for sure if it's, like, objectively there the way I see it or not. I'm just not sure. \n\nHonestly, trying to figure out what's real and what's just me overthinking is basically my entire life right now. Like with the seating chart for the VIP dinner next week. I spent four hours last night staring at this massive spreadsheet, trying to figure out if putting the regional director next to the keynote speaker was a subtle power move or just a logistical necessity because of the dietary restrictions. The keynote is strictly vegan, but the regional director is doing keto, and the venue's kitchen is so small I had to group the special meals near the service doors so the waitstaff doesn't cause a traffic jam. And then I started second-guessing if the service doors are too loud and if it's going to ruin the ambiance during the speeches. I ended up emailing the venue coordinator at 2 AM asking for the decibel rating of their kitchen doors swinging shut. She probably thinks I'm completely unhinged. But if someone's giving a toast and all you hear is plates crashing in the background, who gets blamed? Me. It's always me. \n\nBut, um, anyway... about my face being uneven... yeah, I really don't know for sure. I just know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face looks uneven when you're not looking at pictures of yourself?\n\nPatient: Um, when I'm not looking at pictures? I don't really know... I guess I'm not sure. It's hard to say. \n\nActually, speaking of pictures, you wouldn't believe the absolute nightmare I'm dealing with right now regarding the photo booth vendor for this weekend's corporate retreat. The vendor—who I specifically vetted, by the way—sent over the digital proofs for the printed photo strips yesterday, and they used the complete wrong hex code for the company's logo. It was supposed to be this very specific, cool-toned slate gray, and it printed out looking like this muddy, warm taupe color. I was furious. I mean, branding is literally everything for this client. If the CEO sees taupe on the photo strips, he's going to lose his mind. \n\nSo I had to drop everything and drive all the way out to their warehouse in the middle of rush hour traffic just to look at their printer settings in person. And it turns out they were just using cheap, third-party ink cartridges to cut costs! I had to threaten to pull their contract entirely and blast them on Yelp just to get them to recalibrate the machine and use the right ink. It is just so exhausting having to micromanage grown adults who can't even do the one job they were hired to do. \n\nBut, um, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, my face without pictures. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I just don't know.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your face would look uneven to you even if nobody else noticed it or commented on it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Um, if nobody else noticed it? I mean... I don't know. I really don't. It's so hard to say what I'd see if I wasn't constantly worrying about what other people are seeing, you know? 

Which is exactly the issue I'm having with the floral arrangements for the gala on Saturday. And it completely changes the texture of the centerpieces. Hydrangeas are full and soft, and carnations just look so... cheap and jagged.

She kept saying, "It's just white flowers, nobody is looking that closely." But that's the thing! It doesn't matter if they don't look closely, because the overall effect is ruined. But, um... yeah, about my face... I just really don't know if I'd still see it the same way. I'm just not sure.

The chef literally didn't read my emails. \n\nBut anyway, because I'm running these things now, I have to be in front of people a lot, and they take all these promotional photos. And... I just can't stop staring at this one part of my face in the pictures. It looks so incredibly uneven. It's all I can look at. I've tried pointing it out to my friends and my coworkers, and they always say they barely see anything or that I'm crazy, but it's glaringly obvious to me. It just ruins the whole picture. Kind of like how one bad appetizer tray ruined that whole mixer, you know? But yeah, the photos are... they're really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just in pictures that you notice this unevenness, or do you think about it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it's definitely not just when I see the final pictures. Like, before I even send out any event photos or right before I have to hop on a vendor meeting... Oh, actually, speaking of vendors, I have this one florist, Brenda, who is honestly *so* difficult to work with. She always tries to push these really aggressive neon lilies when I specifically ask for soft pastel peonies. Like, peonies are just so much more elegant for a spring mixer, you know? Why would you want neon floral arrangements at a corporate networking event? It just looks cheap, and I'm the one who gets blamed if the aesthetic is off. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, the unevenness. So before I meet with people like Brenda, or before I send out pictures, I’ll just sit there and keep checking my face in my phone camera. I'll retake photos of myself over and over, or I'll try to tilt my head a certain way to see if I can hide it. Sometimes I'll even pull aside a coworker—usually Jessica, she’s the one who had to help me deal with that awful vegan catering mess last week—and I'll ask her if I look weird. And honestly, doing all that... the checking and asking and tilting... it ends up eating up so much of my time that I actually fall behind on my planning stuff. Which just makes everything more stressful because then vendors start emailing me in all caps asking for updates.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about the unevenness of your face affect your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? I don't know, maybe a little? I mean, I guess I don't really go out as much as I used to. My friend Sarah was just asking me to go to this brunch place downtown this weekend. They do this bottomless mimosa thing, which sounds fun, but the last time we went to a brunch spot, the waitstaff was so incredibly disorganized. Like, they seated us and then didn't bring water for twenty minutes. And as an event planner, I just can't turn that part of my brain off, you know? I'm sitting there watching their whole floor plan fail, and I'm just like, \"If you just moved the host stand to the left, this wouldn't be a bottleneck!\" \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I guess I haven't really noticed it affecting my friends or family too much. I mean, I still talk to them. It's just hard to find time anyway when work is so crazy. Like, my mom called me yesterday right in the middle of a venue walkthrough, and I had to just let it go to voicemail because the lighting guy was trying to tell me we couldn't do the uplighting I wanted. Which was ridiculous, because I specifically checked the power grid specs before we booked the space. So, yeah, I'm not really sure about the unevenness thing outside of work.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when you think about the unevenness of your face?\n\nPatient: Um, distressed? I’m not really sure how to, like, measure that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I guess it's just sort of mixed in with all the other stress I have going on. \n\nLike... actually, speaking of stress, I am currently dealing with the absolute worst vendor situation for this winter gala I'm putting together. It's this guy, DJ Marcus. I've worked with him before, and usually he's totally fine, but he just completely ghosted me after I sent the deposit. His phone goes straight to voicemail now. And it is driving me crazy because I had this very specific 1920s jazz-fusion vibe planned for the cocktail hour, which is crucial because the venue has these gorgeous original Art Deco chandeliers. It took me weeks to secure that space. If I have to hire some last-minute amateur who just blasts top 40 club hits while people are trying to eat mini beef Wellingtons... it’s going to completely destroy the entire ambiance. It is just so hard to find reliable people who actually understand the aesthetic I'm going for. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, about my face. Yeah, I don't know exactly how distressed I am about it. I'm not really sure. I just know it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that thinking about the unevenness of your face is one of the most distressing things you're dealing with right now?\n\nPatient: Um, the most distressing? I don't know if I'd say it's the *most* distressing thing. I mean, it's definitely up there, but... honestly, right now, the most distressing thing is probably this whole seating chart fiasco for the gala. \n\nI was up until 2:00 AM last night trying to figure out where to put the VIP sponsors. Because, okay, so the main sponsor is this tech company, right? And their CEO absolutely hates being seated near the kitchen doors—which I totally get, the swinging doors are super distracting and the waitstaff is always rushing by. But the only other premium tables are near the stage, and the *other* big sponsor, this local real estate firm, specifically requested stage-adjacent seating. And they have this weird rivalry going on, so I can't put them next to each other. It's like a giant puzzle, and if I mess it up, they might pull their funding for next year. I literally had a stress dream about it where all the tables were just floating in the air. \n\nSo, yeah... I don't know if my face is the *most* distressing thing. It's just... I'm not really sure how to rank it with everything else going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the unevenness of your face at times when you're not looking at pictures or getting ready for events?\n\nPatient: Um, when I'm not looking at pictures? I don't really know. I guess I haven't really kept track of when I think about it. It just sort of pops into my head sometimes, but I'm not sure if there's a specific pattern to it. \n\nActually, speaking of pictures, the photographer for the gala just sent over his shot list, and I am so frustrated. He completely ignored my notes about the lighting in the foyer. I specifically told him we need warm fill lights because the marble floors bounce the overheads in this really harsh, unflattering way. And he's like, \"Oh, I'll just fix it in post.\" Like, no! You can't just fix terrible lighting in post when the client is expecting high-end event photography for their annual report. I swear, half my job is just repeating myself to people who think they know better. I even sent him a mood board with exact references for the shadows. I had to spend three hours yesterday on the phone with wholesalers trying to find a substitute that doesn't look cheap. White ranunculus is fine, I guess, but it doesn't have that striking dark center that the anemones have, which was supposed to tie in with the black velvet table runners. It just throws off the whole visual balance of the room. It’s so stressful when you have this perfect vision in your head and people just drop the ball. \n\nBut yeah, about the everyday thing... I really couldn't tell you for sure. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that other people notice the unevenness of your face as much as you do?\n\nPatient: Um, do other people notice it? I can't stop staring at this one uneven part of my face whenever I see event photos. But when I bring it up to my friends or my coworkers, they always say they barely see anything, or that I'm crazy for even noticing it. \n\nWhich is exactly what the catering manager said to me yesterday when I told him the fondant on the dessert bites was a shade too yellow! He was like, \"Nobody is going to notice that, it's just white.\" And I had to explain to him that there is a huge difference between ivory and buttercream, especially when they're sitting on a stark white porcelain tray. It just looks like they've gone bad or something. I mean, the lighting at the venue is already going to wash things out, so if the undertones are wrong, the whole dessert table is going to look cheap. I had to literally hold up a pantone swatch to the petit fours to prove my point. I mean... I don't really know. Like, obviously I see it, but I guess I can't say for sure if it's, like, objectively there the way I see it or not. I'm just not sure. \n\nHonestly, trying to figure out what's real and what's just me overthinking is basically my entire life right now. Like with the seating chart for the VIP dinner next week. Um, if a doctor told me it was normal? I mean... I guess I'm not sure. Like, would I believe them? I don't know... it's hard to trust people when they tell you things are fine, you know? 

Like with the catering company for this upcoming tech launch. The head chef kept telling me, "It's fine, it's fine, the appetizers will be ready on time," but I knew they wouldn't be. I just knew it. I kept looking at his prep schedule, and he had only allocated 45 minutes to assemble 300 miniature beef Wellingtons. I'm sorry, but unless you have an army of culinary students in the back, that is physically impossible. I told him he needed to start prep earlier, and he just brushed me off, saying, "I'm a professional, I know what I'm doing." 

Well, guess what? We did a dry run yesterday, and he was 20 minutes late getting the first tray out. Twenty minutes! At a tech launch, 20 minutes is an eternity. People get bored, they start looking at their phones, and the whole energy of the room just dies. I had to sit down with him and literally map out a minute-by-minute timeline for his kitchen staff, down to when they take the puff pastry out of the fridge so it doesn't get too warm. He was so annoyed with me, but I don't care. If those Wellingtons aren't perfect, it's my reputation on the line, not his. 

But, um... sorry. What were you asking? Oh, right, the plastic surgeon thing. Yeah, I don't really know if I'd see it differently or not. I just don't know.

But when I bring it up to my friends or my coworkers, they always say they barely see anything, or that I'm crazy for even noticing it. \n\nWhich is exactly what the catering manager said to me yesterday when I told him the fondant on the dessert bites was a shade too yellow! He was like, \"Nobody is going to notice that, it's just white.\" And I had to explain to him that there is a huge difference between ivory and buttercream, especially when they're sitting on a stark white porcelain tray. I mean... I don't really know. Like, obviously I see it, but I guess I can't say for sure if it's, like, objectively there the way I see it or not. I'm just not sure. \n\nHonestly, trying to figure out what's real and what's just me overthinking is basically my entire life right now. Like with the seating chart for the VIP dinner next week. And then I started second-guessing if the service doors are too loud and if it's going to ruin the ambiance during the speeches. It was supposed to be this very specific, cool-toned slate gray, and it printed out looking like this muddy, warm taupe color. I was furious. I mean, branding is literally everything for this client. If the CEO sees taupe on the photo strips, he's going to lose his mind. \n\nSo I had to drop everything and drive all the way out to their warehouse in the middle of rush hour traffic just to look at their printer settings in person. And it turns out they were just using cheap, third-party ink cartridges to cut costs! I had to threaten to pull their contract entirely and blast them on Yelp just to get them to recalibrate the machine and use the right ink. It is just so exhausting having to micromanage grown adults who can't even do the one job they were hired to do. \n\nBut, um, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, my face without pictures. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look uneven to you even if nobody else noticed it or commented on it?\n\nPatient: Um, if nobody else noticed it? I mean... I don't know. I really don't. It's so hard to say what I'd see if I wasn't constantly worrying about what other people are seeing, you know? \n\nWhich is exactly the issue I'm having with the floral arrangements for the gala on Saturday. The florist tried to tell me that nobody is going to notice if we swap out the white hydrangeas for white carnations to save on the budget. And I'm like, are you kidding me? Carnations? For a black-tie gala? It's not a high school prom! I don't care if the guests don't know the difference between a hydrangea and a carnation—I know the difference. And it completely changes the texture of the centerpieces. Hydrangeas are full and soft, and carnations just look so... cheap and jagged. \n\nI literally spent an hour on the phone with her yesterday, begging her to just find a wholesaler who could rush-deliver the hydrangeas. She kept saying, \"It's just white flowers, nobody is looking that closely.\" But that's the thing! It doesn't matter if they don't look closely, because the overall effect is ruined. If the centerpieces look cheap, the whole room feels cheap, and then the client thinks I don't know what I'm doing. I ended up having to pull from the audiovisual budget just to cover the difference in the floral costs, which means now I have to figure out how to light the stage with two fewer spotlights, and I am stressed out of my mind about it. \n\nBut, um... yeah, about my face... I just really don't know if I'd still see it the same way. I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look uneven even if a plastic surgeon or a medical professional told you that it's perfectly normal and there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Um, if a doctor told me it was normal? Um, not as noticeable? I mean... I guess maybe? But that's kind of why I came in to see you in the first place. I just can't stop staring at this one part of my face in the pictures. It looks so incredibly uneven. It's all I can look at. Kind of like how one bad appetizer tray ruined that whole mixer, you know? But yeah, the photos are... they're really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just in pictures that you notice this unevenness, or do you think about it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it's definitely not just when I see the final pictures. Like, before I even send out any event photos or right before I have to hop on a vendor meeting... Oh, actually, speaking of vendors, I have this one florist, Brenda, who is honestly *so* difficult to work with. She always tries to push these really aggressive neon lilies when I specifically ask for soft pastel peonies. Like, peonies are just so much more elegant for a spring mixer, you know? Why would you want neon floral arrangements at a corporate networking event? It just looks cheap, and I'm the one who gets blamed if the aesthetic is off. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, the unevenness. So before I meet with people like Brenda, or before I send out pictures, I’ll just sit there and keep checking my face in my phone camera. I'll retake photos of myself over and over, or I'll try to tilt my head a certain way to see if I can hide it. Sometimes I'll even pull aside a coworker—usually Jessica, she’s the one who had to help me deal with that awful vegan catering mess last week—and I'll ask her if I look weird. And honestly, doing all that... the checking and asking and tilting... it ends up eating up so much of my time that I actually fall behind on my planning stuff. Which just makes everything more stressful because then vendors start emailing me in all caps asking for updates.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about the unevenness of your face affect your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? I don't know, maybe a little? I mean, I guess I don't really go out as much as I used to. My friend Sarah was just asking me to go to this brunch place downtown this weekend. They do this bottomless mimosa thing, which sounds fun, but the last time we went to a brunch spot, the waitstaff was so incredibly disorganized. Like, they seated us and then didn't bring water for twenty minutes. And as an event planner, I just can't turn that part of my brain off, you know? I'm sitting there watching their whole floor plan fail, and I'm just like, \"If you just moved the host stand to the left, this wouldn't be a bottleneck!\" \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I guess I haven't really noticed it affecting my friends or family too much. I mean, I still talk to them. It's just hard to find time anyway when work is so crazy. Like, my mom called me yesterday right in the middle of a venue walkthrough, and I had to just let it go to voicemail because the lighting guy was trying to tell me we couldn't do the uplighting I wanted. Which was ridiculous, because I specifically checked the power grid specs before we booked the space. So, yeah, I'm not really sure about the unevenness thing outside of work.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when you think about the unevenness of your face?\n\nPatient: Um, distressed? I’m not really sure how to, like, measure that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I guess it's just sort of mixed in with all the other stress I have going on. \n\nLike... actually, speaking of stress, I am currently dealing with the absolute worst vendor situation for this winter gala I'm putting together. I don't know if I'd say it's the *most* distressing thing. Because, okay, so the main sponsor is this tech company, right? And their CEO absolutely hates being seated near the kitchen doors—which I totally get, the swinging doors are super distracting and the waitstaff is always rushing by. But the only other premium tables are near the stage, and the *other* big sponsor, this local real estate firm, specifically requested stage-adjacent seating. And they have this weird rivalry going on, so I can't put them next to each other. It's like a giant puzzle, and if I mess it up, they might pull their funding for next year. I literally had a stress dream about it where all the tables were just floating in the air. \n\nSo, yeah... I don't know if my face is the *most* distressing thing. It's just... I'm not really sure how to rank it with everything else going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the unevenness of your face at times when you're not looking at pictures or getting ready for events?\n\nPatient: Um, when I'm not looking at pictures? I don't really know. I guess I haven't really kept track of when I think about it. It just sort of pops into my head sometimes, but I'm not sure if there's a specific pattern to it. \n\nActually, speaking of pictures, the photographer for the gala just sent over his shot list, and I am so frustrated. He completely ignored my notes about the lighting in the foyer. I specifically told him we need warm fill lights because the marble floors bounce the overheads in this really harsh, unflattering way. And he's like, \"Oh, I'll just fix it in post.\" Like, no! You can't just fix terrible lighting in post when the client is expecting high-end event photography for their annual report. I swear, half my job is just repeating myself to people who think they know better. I even sent him a mood board with exact references for the shadows. It's just exhausting having to micromanage professionals, you know? \n\nBut, um, to answer your question... yeah, I'm not really sure if I think about my face when I'm not getting ready or whatever. I haven't really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the unevenness of your face every day?\n\nPatient: Um, every day? I don't know... maybe? I really haven't been keeping a tally or anything. It's just hard to say. \n\nLike, my days just blur together lately anyway because of this floral crisis. The florist I hired—who I thought was amazing—just told me she can't get the white anemones I ordered for the centerpieces because of some supply chain issue. And I'm like, how do you not know this until a week before the event? The whole color palette is based around those specific flowers! I had to spend three hours yesterday on the phone with wholesalers trying to find a substitute that doesn't look cheap. White ranunculus is fine, I guess, but it doesn't have that striking dark center that the anemones have, which was supposed to tie in with the black velvet table runners. It just throws off the whole visual balance of the room. It’s so stressful when you have this perfect vision in your head and people just drop the ball. \n\nBut yeah, about the everyday thing... I really couldn't tell you for sure. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that other people notice the unevenness of your face as much as you do?\n\nPatient: Um, do other people notice it? Well, that's actually kind of why I'm here. Because ever since I got promoted and I have to be the one standing at the front of the room, directing the staff and greeting the clients, I just... I can't stop staring at this one uneven part of my face whenever I see event photos. But when I bring it up to my friends or my coworkers, they always say they barely see anything, or that I'm crazy for even noticing it. \n\nWhich is exactly what the catering manager said to me yesterday when I told him the fondant on the dessert bites was a shade too yellow! He was like, \"Nobody is going to notice that, it's just white.\" And I had to explain to him that there is a huge difference between ivory and buttercream, especially when they're sitting on a stark white porcelain tray. It just looks like they've gone bad or something. I mean, the lighting at the venue is already going to wash things out, so if the undertones are wrong, the whole dessert table is going to look cheap. I had to literally hold up a pantone swatch to the petit fours to prove my point. People just do not pay attention to detail, and it drives me insane. \n\nBut yeah, with my face... people say they don't really see it, even though I can't stop looking at it in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that the unevenness of your face is actually there, even if other people don't notice it?\n\nPatient: Um, is it actually there? I mean... I don't really know. Like, obviously I see it, but I guess I can't say for sure if it's, like, objectively there the way I see it or not. I'm just not sure. \n\nHonestly, trying to figure out what's real and what's just me overthinking is basically my entire life right now. Like with the seating chart for the VIP dinner next week. I spent four hours last night staring at this massive spreadsheet, trying to figure out if putting the regional director next to the keynote speaker was a subtle power move or just a logistical necessity because of the dietary restrictions. The keynote is strictly vegan, but the regional director is doing keto, and the venue's kitchen is so small I had to group the special meals near the service doors so the waitstaff doesn't cause a traffic jam. And then I started second-guessing if the service doors are too loud and if it's going to ruin the ambiance during the speeches. I ended up emailing the venue coordinator at 2 AM asking for the decibel rating of their kitchen doors swinging shut. She probably thinks I'm completely unhinged. But if someone's giving a toast and all you hear is plates crashing in the background, who gets blamed? Me. It's always me. \n\nBut, um, anyway... about my face being uneven... yeah, I really don't know for sure. I just know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face looks uneven when you're not looking at pictures of yourself?\n\nPatient: Um, when I'm not looking at pictures? I don't really know... I guess I'm not sure. It's hard to say. \n\nActually, speaking of pictures, you wouldn't believe the absolute nightmare I'm dealing with right now regarding the photo booth vendor for this weekend's corporate retreat. The vendor—who I specifically vetted, by the way—sent over the digital proofs for the printed photo strips yesterday, and they used the complete wrong hex code for the company's logo. It was supposed to be this very specific, cool-toned slate gray, and it printed out looking like this muddy, warm taupe color. I was furious. I mean, branding is literally everything for this client. If the CEO sees taupe on the photo strips, he's going to lose his mind. \n\nSo I had to drop everything and drive all the way out to their warehouse in the middle of rush hour traffic just to look at their printer settings in person. And it turns out they were just using cheap, third-party ink cartridges to cut costs! I had to threaten to pull their contract entirely and blast them on Yelp just to get them to recalibrate the machine and use the right ink. It is just so exhausting having to micromanage grown adults who can't even do the one job they were hired to do. \n\nBut, um, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, my face without pictures. Yeah, I don't really have a good answer for that. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look uneven to you even if nobody else noticed it or commented on it?\n\nPatient: Um, if nobody else noticed it? I mean... I don't know. I really don't. It's so hard to say what I'd see if I wasn't constantly worrying about what other people are seeing, you know? \n\nWhich is exactly the issue I'm having with the floral arrangements for the gala on Saturday. The florist tried to tell me that nobody is going to notice if we swap out the white hydrangeas for white carnations to save on the budget. And I'm like, are you kidding me? Carnations? For a black-tie gala? It's not a high school prom! I don't care if the guests don't know the difference between a hydrangea and a carnation—I know the difference. And it completely changes the texture of the centerpieces. Hydrangeas are full and soft, and carnations just look so... cheap and jagged. \n\nI literally spent an hour on the phone with her yesterday, begging her to just find a wholesaler who could rush-deliver the hydrangeas. She kept saying, \"It's just white flowers, nobody is looking that closely.\" But that's the thing! It doesn't matter if they don't look closely, because the overall effect is ruined. If the centerpieces look cheap, the whole room feels cheap, and then the client thinks I don't know what I'm doing. I ended up having to pull from the audiovisual budget just to cover the difference in the floral costs, which means now I have to figure out how to light the stage with two fewer spotlights, and I am stressed out of my mind about it. \n\nBut, um... yeah, about my face... I just really don't know if I'd still see it the same way. I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look uneven even if a plastic surgeon or a medical professional told you that it's perfectly normal and there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Um, if a doctor told me it was normal? I mean... I guess I'm not sure. Like, would I believe them? I don't know... it's hard to trust people when they tell you things are fine, you know? \n\nLike with the catering company for this upcoming tech launch. The head chef kept telling me, \"It's fine, it's fine, the appetizers will be ready on time,\" but I knew they wouldn't be. I just knew it. I kept looking at his prep schedule, and he had only allocated 45 minutes to assemble 300 miniature beef Wellingtons. I'm sorry, but unless you have an army of culinary students in the back, that is physically impossible. I told him he needed to start prep earlier, and he just brushed me off, saying, \"I'm a professional, I know what I'm doing.\" \n\nWell, guess what? We did a dry run yesterday, and he was 20 minutes late getting the first tray out. Twenty minutes! At a tech launch, 20 minutes is an eternity. People get bored, they start looking at their phones, and the whole energy of the room just dies. I had to sit down with him and literally map out a minute-by-minute timeline for his kitchen staff, down to when they take the puff pastry out of the fridge so it doesn't get too warm. He was so annoyed with me, but I don't care. If those Wellingtons aren't perfect, it's my reputation on the line, not his. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What were you asking? Oh, right, the plastic surgeon thing. Yeah, I don't really know if I'd see it differently or not. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that the unevenness you see in your face might not be as noticeable or present as you think it is?\n\nPatient: Um, not as noticeable? I mean... I guess maybe? But that's kind of why I came in to see you in the first place. Ever since I got promoted and I have to be the one actually out on the floor managing these events, being in front of all these clients and vendors... I just can't stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face whenever I see photos from the events. Even when everyone else tells me they barely see anything, I just... I can't stop focusing on it. \n\nWhich is honestly so frustrating, because I really need to be focusing on the VIP gift bags for the charity auction next month. The supplier just informed me that the custom silk ribbons I ordered for the boxes are stuck in customs, and they want to substitute them with satin ribbons. Satin! Can you believe that? Satin is so shiny and cheap-looking compared to silk. It completely ruins the elegant, understated vibe we're going for. I had to spend three hours on the phone this morning trying to track down a domestic supplier who can expedite a hundred yards of French silk ribbon, and they are charging me double the original price. I had to pull from the entertainment budget just to cover it, and now I'm worried the string quartet is going to be short a cellist. It's just one disaster after another. \n\nBut, yeah, um... what you were saying about my face... I don't know, it's just really hard for me to believe it's not as noticeable as I think it is, even if people say that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Um, if I couldn't see any pictures? I mean... I guess I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it like that, mostly because I'm just surrounded by photos all the time, you know? 

Which actually reminds me of the photographer we hired for the winter corporate retreat. Oh my gosh, I am so furious with him right now. We had a strict shot list, right? I sent it to him three weeks ago. I explicitly told him we needed wide, sweeping shots of the ballroom before anyone walked in, to capture the uplighting and the table settings untouched. And what does he do? He shows up late, totally misses the empty room window, and just starts taking all these super close-up, harsh flash photos of people eating. Like, who wants a picture of themselves chewing a dinner roll? 

I had to literally chase him around the venue in my heels and physically point at the ice sculpture to get him to photograph it before it started melting. And the lighting he used completely washed out the blue LEDs we had installed under the ice! I spent a huge chunk of the client's budget on that sculpture, and now they won't even have a decent picture of it for their annual report. I had to stay up until 2 AM last night trying to color-correct the raw files myself because his edits were just... awful. It's like if I don't micromanage every single person on my vendor list, everything just falls apart. 

But, um... sorry, I'm rambling. You were asking about pictures of me? Yeah, I don't know. I really couldn't tell you if the unevenness would still be there if I didn't see it in photos. I'm just not sure.

Um, if a bunch of experts told me it was fine? I... I honestly don't know if it would still bother me or not. I'm just not sure. Because, you know, people say they're experts all the time, but...

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, if I couldn't see myself in mirrors? I guess... I mean, the whole reason I came in is because I've had to be more in front of people at work lately, and I just can't stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos. Even when everyone else says they barely see anything, I just... I can't stop focusing on it. But if there were no mirrors or reflections? I don't know. 

It kind of makes me think about this venue I scouted last month for a charity gala. It was this gorgeous, historic mansion, right? But the client was absolutely obsessed with having these massive, mirrored tables for the dining area. I told her, I said, "Look, mirrored tables are a nightmare. Every single fingerprint, every water ring, every time someone drops a fork, it's going to show." But she insisted. She had this whole vision of the centerpieces reflecting infinitely or whatever. 

So, I had to track down this specialty rental company three states over just to source forty of these mirrored tables. And let me tell you, the transport alone was a logistical nightmare. Two of them cracked in transit, and I had to spend four hours on the phone with the freight company trying to get a rush replacement before the event. And then, on the actual night of the gala, the lighting designer completely messed up the angles, and the spotlights were just bouncing off the tables straight into the guests' eyes! It was blinding. I spent half the night running around asking the crew to adjust the rigging just so people could eat without getting a migraine. It's like, sometimes people get so fixated on one specific detail that they completely ignore how it actually functions in reality. 

But, um... sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Going off track. You were asking about if I couldn't see reflections? Yeah, I mean, I guess if I couldn't see it, maybe it wouldn't bother me as much? But right now, with all the photos and being in front of people, it's just really hard to ignore that unevenness.

For a black-tie gala? It's not a high school prom! I don't care if the guests don't know the difference between a hydrangea and a carnation—I know the difference. Hydrangeas are full and soft, and carnations just look so... cheap and jagged. \n\nI literally spent an hour on the phone with her yesterday, begging her to just find a wholesaler who could rush-deliver the hydrangeas. She kept saying, \"It's just white flowers, nobody is looking that closely.\" But that's the thing! It doesn't matter if they don't look closely, because the overall effect is ruined. If the centerpieces look cheap, the whole room feels cheap, and then the client thinks I don't know what I'm doing. I ended up having to pull from the audiovisual budget just to cover the difference in the floral costs, which means now I have to figure out how to light the stage with two fewer spotlights, and I am stressed out of my mind about it. \n\nBut, um... yeah, about my face... I just really don't know if I'd still see it the same way. I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look uneven even if a plastic surgeon or a medical professional told you that it's perfectly normal and there's nothing wrong with it?\n\nPatient: Um, if a doctor told me it was normal? I mean... I guess I'm not sure. Like, would I believe them? I don't know... it's hard to trust people when they tell you things are fine, you know? \n\nLike with the catering company for this upcoming tech launch. The head chef kept telling me, \"It's fine, it's fine, the appetizers will be ready on time,\" but I knew they wouldn't be. I just knew it. I kept looking at his prep schedule, and he had only allocated 45 minutes to assemble 300 miniature beef Wellingtons. I'm sorry, but unless you have an army of culinary students in the back, that is physically impossible. I told him he needed to start prep earlier, and he just brushed me off, saying, \"I'm a professional, I know what I'm doing.\" \n\nWell, guess what? We did a dry run yesterday, and he was 20 minutes late getting the first tray out. Twenty minutes! At a tech launch, 20 minutes is an eternity. I had to sit down with him and literally map out a minute-by-minute timeline for his kitchen staff, down to when they take the puff pastry out of the fridge so it doesn't get too warm. He was so annoyed with me, but I don't care. If those Wellingtons aren't perfect, it's my reputation on the line, not his. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What were you asking? Oh, right, the plastic surgeon thing. Yeah, I don't really know if I'd see it differently or not. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that the unevenness you see in your face might not be as noticeable or present as you think it is?\n\nPatient: Um, not as noticeable? I mean... I guess maybe? But that's kind of why I came in to see you in the first place. Ever since I got promoted and I have to be the one actually out on the floor managing these events, being in front of all these clients and vendors... Um, that I'm just perceiving it that way? I... I don't know. Like, is my brain just making it up? I'm not sure if that's even possible. 

It kind of reminds me of this optical illusion thing we had at a corporate retreat I planned back in October. The whole theme was "shifting perspectives," which, honestly, was a little cheesy, but the client loved it. Anyway, they hired this interactive artist who set up these crazy installations in the lobby of the hotel. There was this one room where the floor was painted with these geometric patterns, and if you stood in one specific corner, it looked like a giant, 3D hole was opening up in the ground. It was wild. People were literally stepping around it because it looked so real, even though it was totally flat. 

But the problem was, the artist insisted on using this special matte paint that took forever to dry, and he didn't finish until like, two hours before the guests arrived. I was panicking. I had to rope off the entire section and stand there like a bouncer, telling all the hotel staff not to walk on it. And then, during the actual event, someone spilled a tray of red wine right in the middle of the "hole." It completely ruined the illusion. The artist was furious, the catering manager was crying, and I had to spend the rest of the night trying to find a rug big enough to cover the stain without making it look obvious. It was just a total disaster. 

But, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, about my face being even. Yeah, I just... I really don't know if it's just my perception. I don't think I can answer that.

Kind of like how one bad appetizer tray ruined that whole mixer, you know? But yeah, the photos are... they're really bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just in pictures that you notice this unevenness, or do you think about it at other times as well?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it's definitely not just when I see the final pictures. Like, before I even send out any event photos or right before I have to hop on a vendor meeting... Oh, actually, speaking of vendors, I have this one florist, Brenda, who is honestly *so* difficult to work with. She always tries to push these really aggressive neon lilies when I specifically ask for soft pastel peonies. Like, peonies are just so much more elegant for a spring mixer, you know? Why would you want neon floral arrangements at a corporate networking event? It just looks cheap, and I'm the one who gets blamed if the aesthetic is off. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, the unevenness. So before I meet with people like Brenda, or before I send out pictures, I’ll just sit there and keep checking my face in my phone camera. I'll retake photos of myself over and over, or I'll try to tilt my head a certain way to see if I can hide it. Sometimes I'll even pull aside a coworker—usually Jessica, she’s the one who had to help me deal with that awful vegan catering mess last week—and I'll ask her if I look weird. And honestly, doing all that... the checking and asking and tilting... it ends up eating up so much of my time that I actually fall behind on my planning stuff. Which just makes everything more stressful because then vendors start emailing me in all caps asking for updates.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about the unevenness of your face affect your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? I don't know, maybe a little? I mean, I guess I don't really go out as much as I used to. My friend Sarah was just asking me to go to this brunch place downtown this weekend. They do this bottomless mimosa thing, which sounds fun, but the last time we went to a brunch spot, the waitstaff was so incredibly disorganized. Like, they seated us and then didn't bring water for twenty minutes. And as an event planner, I just can't turn that part of my brain off, you know? I'm sitting there watching their whole floor plan fail, and I'm just like, \"If you just moved the host stand to the left, this wouldn't be a bottleneck!\" \n\nBut yeah, I don't know. I guess I haven't really noticed it affecting my friends or family too much. I mean, I still talk to them. It's just hard to find time anyway when work is so crazy. Like, my mom called me yesterday right in the middle of a venue walkthrough, and I had to just let it go to voicemail because the lighting guy was trying to tell me we couldn't do the uplighting I wanted. Which was ridiculous, because I specifically checked the power grid specs before we booked the space. So, yeah, I'm not really sure about the unevenness thing outside of work.\n\nDoctor: How distressed do you feel when you think about the unevenness of your face?\n\nPatient: Um, distressed? I’m not really sure how to, like, measure that. I haven't really thought about it in those terms. I guess it's just sort of mixed in with all the other stress I have going on. \n\nLike... actually, speaking of stress, I am currently dealing with the absolute worst vendor situation for this winter gala I'm putting together. It's this guy, DJ Marcus. I've worked with him before, and usually he's totally fine, but he just completely ghosted me after I sent the deposit. His phone goes straight to voicemail now. And it is driving me crazy because I had this very specific 1920s jazz-fusion vibe planned for the cocktail hour, which is crucial because the venue has these gorgeous original Art Deco chandeliers. It took me weeks to secure that space. If I have to hire some last-minute amateur who just blasts top 40 club hits while people are trying to eat mini beef Wellingtons... it’s going to completely destroy the entire ambiance. It is just so hard to find reliable people who actually understand the aesthetic I'm going for. \n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, about my face. I don't know if I'd say it's the *most* distressing thing. Because, okay, so the main sponsor is this tech company, right? And their CEO absolutely hates being seated near the kitchen doors—which I totally get, the swinging doors are super distracting and the waitstaff is always rushing by. But the only other premium tables are near the stage, and the *other* big sponsor, this local real estate firm, specifically requested stage-adjacent seating. And they have this weird rivalry going on, so I can't put them next to each other. It's like a giant puzzle, and if I mess it up, they might pull their funding for next year. I literally had a stress dream about it where all the tables were just floating in the air. \n\nSo, yeah... I don't know if my face is the *most* distressing thing. It's just... I'm not really sure how to rank it with everything else going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the unevenness of your face at times when you're not looking at pictures or getting ready for events?\n\nPatient: Um, when I'm not looking at pictures? I don't really know. I guess I haven't really kept track of when I think about it. It just sort of pops into my head sometimes, but I'm not sure if there's a specific pattern to it. \n\nActually, speaking of pictures, the photographer for the gala just sent over his shot list, and I am so frustrated. He completely ignored my notes about the lighting in the foyer. I specifically told him we need warm fill lights because the marble floors bounce the overheads in this really harsh, unflattering way. And he's like, \"Oh, I'll just fix it in post.\" Like, no! You can't just fix terrible lighting in post when the client is expecting high-end event photography for their annual report.
